## Page 1


THE SPECTRUM OF ANOSOV REPRESENTATIONS
YANNICK GUEDES BONTHONNEAU, THIBAULT LEFEUVRE, AND TOBIAS WEICH
Abstract. Given a ϑ-Anosov representation into a real reductive group G,
we construct a natural resonance spectrum associated with the representation.
This spectrum is a complex analytic variety of codimension 1 in (a∗
ϑ)C, the
complexiﬁed dual of the split component of the associated Levi group Lϑ < G.
We reinterpret several objects from the theory of Anosov representations
within this spectral framework and investigate, in higher rank, questions that
are classically related to Ruelle–Pollicott theory in the rank-one setting. In
particular, the “leading resonance”—which is now a hypersurface—is identiﬁed
with the critical hypersurface of the representation.
As a corollary of our work, we prove that the zeta functions and Poincaré
series associated with Anosov representations admit a meromorphic extension
to (a∗
ϑ)C. We also establish sharp mixing estimates for the refraction ﬂow under
a Diophantine condition on the representation. Most of our results concerning
Anosov representations are obtained as a byproduct of a general theory of free
Abelian cocycles over hyperbolic ﬂows.
This article is intended as a foundational work toward more advanced re-
sults such as higher-rank quantum/classical correspondence, the detection of
topological invariants of representations via the value at zero of Poincaré se-
ries or the order of vanishing of zeta functions, sharp counting results for the
Lyapunov spectrum, etc.

GUEDES BONTHONNEAU, LEFEUVRE, AND WEICH
1. Introduction
The purpose of this paper is to lay the foundations for a higher rank spectral
theory for Anosov representations, based on microlocal analysis, and collect the
main consequences. The main results of the present article are stated in §1.3
and §1.4. For readers not familiar with Anosov representations or the theory of
semisimple Lie groups, the necessary Lie-theoretic background can be found in
§8. We also encourage such readers to consider the case G = SLn(R) throughout.
1.1. Anosov representations. Let Γ be a torsion free ﬁnitely generated group,
| • | be a word length on Γ chosen with respect to some ﬁxed generating set. Let
ρ : Γ →G be a discrete and faithful representation. We ﬁrst recall the notion of
Anosov representation:
Deﬁnition 1.1 (ϑ-Anosov representation). Let ϑ ⊂Σ be a non-empty subset of
simple roots. The representation ρ : Γ →G is ϑ-Anosov if there exist positive
constants C1, C2 > 0 such that for all γ ∈Γ and α ∈ϑ:
α (µ(ρ(γ))) ≥C1|γ| −C2.
If ϑ = Σ, we say that the representation is Borel Anosov.
This deﬁnition is not the original one from Labourie [Lab06], but it was
shown to be equivalent, see [GW12, GGKW17, KLP17, BPS19] among other
references. The Anosov property implies that Γ is word-hyperbolic, see [KLP18,
Theorem 1.4] or [BPS19, Theorem 3.2]. For readers who are not familiar with
Lie-theoretic terminology, we encourage to consider the case ϑ = Σ and G =
SLn(R) throughout. We also emphasize that, in the core of the article, Anosov
representations will be indexed by a generalized Weyl chamber C ⊂a rather
than by a subset ϑ of simple roots; this choice will be more convenient for several
purposes (see Remark 8.3 for further discussion). However, for the purpose of
this introduction, we stick to the more standard indexing. We also note that
our results hold for general real reductive Lie groups (see §8), although in this
introduction we restrict to the semisimple case.
Example 1.2. When Γ := π1(Σ), and Σ is an orientable surface of genus
≥2, natural examples of Anosov representations are obtained by deforma-
tion of a representation ρ : π1(Σ) →SLn(R) obtained as ρ := ι ◦ρ0, where
ρ0 : π1(Σ) →SL2(R) is a discrete and faithful representation (hyperbolic struc-
ture on Σ) and ι : SL2(R) →SLn(R) is the (unique) irreducible representation


## Page 5


THE SPECTRUM OF ANOSOV REPRESENTATIONS
5
(obtained explicitly as the action of SL2(R) on homogeneous polynomials of de-
gree n −1 on R2). Such representations are called Hitchin representations and
belong to what is known as the Hitchin component of the moduli space of repre-
sentations Hom(π1(Σ), SLn(R))/ ∼(where ∼denotes equivalence modulo inner
automorphisms of SLn(R)). In this case, the Cartan projection of a matrix is
the set of decreasingly ordered singular values. Hitchin representations are Borel
Anosov.
Example 1.3. As another elementary example, let G = SO(n + 1, 1). This is a
group of rank 1, thus a trivial example in the framework of our paper. In this
case, µ(g) is identiﬁed with the real number µ(g) = dHn+1(o, g.o), where o is an
origin in the hyperbolic space, and λ(g) is the length of the unique primitive
closed geodesic in ⟨γ⟩\Hn. In higher rank, µ should be thought of as a vectorial
notion of distance in the corresponding symmetric space, and λ as the higher
rank length of a closed geodesic.
1.2. Domains of discontinuity. One of the goals of the present paper is the
study of dynamical and/or geometric objects associated with the Anosov rep-
resentation via the introduction of a suitable notion of spectrum (see §1.3 and
§1.4). Most of the objects we are interested in can be described in terms of a
partially hyperbolic dynamical system (an Rk+1-action) which we now explain.
1.2.1. Phase space dynamics. Let
aϑ :=

Anosov representation, aϑ = a.
Let Pϑ < G be the parabolic group corresponding to the subset of roots
ϑ, and let Fϑ := G/Pϑ be the associated partial ﬂag space. If G = SLn(R),
and ϑ = Σ (Borel case), then Pϑ can be chosen as the space of upper triangular
matrices (with determinant 1) and Fϑ corresponds to the space of complete ﬂags
in Rn. We refer to §8.6 for further discussion on this case.
Let Mϑ be the Levi component of Pϑ, ι : a →a the opposite involution and
F (2) ⊂Fϑ × Fι(ϑ) be the open subset of transverse ﬂags (Mϑ = M = diag(±1) ∩
SLn(R) in the Borel case when G = SLn(R)). The Hopf parametrization provides
a natural diﬀeomorphism

Ω⊂G/Mϑ is an appropriate open subset on which Γ acts properly.

This should be thought of a higher rank analogue of the “trapped set” in rank 1
(the set of unit vectors generating a geodesic trapped in a compact region of the
manifold) but the main diﬀerence is that it is now noncompact. It is the relevant
set for the dynamics of the Aϑ-action.
Finally, we consider the Benoist cone
L := {tλϑ(ρ(γ)) : t > 0, γ ∈Γ} ⊂aϑ.
Given a hyperplane h ⊂aϑ, we say that it is transverse to L if
h ∩L = {0}.
If Γ < G is Zariski dense, then L is open, see [Ben97].
1.2.3. Domain of discontinuity. The essential basis for most results contained in
the present article is the following theorem asserting that there exists an open
neighborhood ΩDMS ⊂G/Mϑ of
˜
J , which we call a DMS domain, invariant by
the left Γ-action and right Aϑ-action, and such that Γleft × Hright has a proper
free action, where H := exp(h) and h ⊂aϑ is any hyperplane transverse to the
Benoist cone L . The theorem below was ﬁrst established by Delarue, Monclair
and Sanders in [DMS24, DMS25]; we give a self contained proof in the present
article using a diﬀerent toolbox and partially increasing its generality. We refer
to §11 and Theorem 11.12 below for the full statements. For now, let us state:
Theorem 1.4 (Existence of a domain of discontinuity). Let ρ : Γ →G be a
ϑ-Anosov representation as deﬁned above. The following holds:
(i) There exists an open subset ΩDMS ⊂G/Mϑ, invariant by the left Γ-action
and the right Aϑ-action, containing
˜
J .
(ii) Letting M := Γ\ΩDMS, any hyperplane h ⊂aϑ transverse to L acts freely
and properly on M.
(iii) The group Aϑ/ exp(h) ≃R acts on N := M/ exp(h) as an Axiom A ﬂow
with K := J / exp(h) as single basic set.
The main consequence of Theorem 1.4 is that the full Aϑ-action on M can
be factored as a free Abelian cocycle over an Axiom A ﬂow; see §1.6 where this
is further detailed. This structure will be key to the results described in §1.3 and
§1.4.
Finally, we introduce the incoming (-) and outgoing (+) tails:
Γ± := Γ\{(ξ−, ξ+, a) ∈G/Mϑ : ξ± ∈Λ±}.

We emphasize at this stage that the existence of a suitable neighborhood
of the trapped set J is essential even to deﬁne the resonance spectrum, since
distributions are only deﬁned on open sets. The set J was already identiﬁed
by Sambarino [Sam14] as an important set for the dynamics of the A-action, but
the existence of such a neighborhood was not established there.

Since TϕC(d) ⊕Rϕ = a∗

We are interested in the ergodic
properties of the ﬂow (etuϕ)t∈R generated by uϕ on the trapped set J . This ﬂow
is called Sambarino’s refraction ﬂow.2
There is a natural measure µϕ of inﬁnite mass, preserved by (etuϕ)t∈R and,
more generally, by the full Aϑ-action, with support equal to J , which plays the
role of a measure of maximal entropy in this problem. More precisely, ker(ϕ) ⊂aϑ
has a proper action on J (Theorem 1.4, item (ii)) and
π : J →Kϕ,
Kϕ := J / exp(ker(ϕ)),
is a principal ker(ϕ)-bundle above a compact metric space. The ﬂow (etuϕ)t∈R
descends to Kϕ and yields a (metric) Anosov ﬂow (φϕ
t )t∈R (Theorem 1.4, item

The measure µϕ is then given by µϕ = volker ϕ ∧π∗νϕ, where νϕ is the

We are interested in the decay of correlations for the refraction ﬂow with
respect to µϕ. The next statement involves a notion of Diophantine represen-
tation, which we refrain from introducing here due to its technicality. Roughly
speaking, we say that a representation is Diophantine if there exists a ﬁnite sub-
set of its Lyapunov spectrum (see §2.4.1) which is Diophantine, that is badly
approximable by rational numbers (see Deﬁnition 2.14 for a precise statement).
In certain cases, this is easily seen to be a generic condition with respect to the
representation (Proposition D.2). For instance, we show that Lebesgue almost
every Hitchin representation ρ : π1(Σ) →SLn(R) (Example 1.2) is Diophantine
provided 2g ≥n3, where g is the genus of Σ, see the discussion after Proposi-
tion D.2. More generally, we believe that very few (in a measure-theoretic sense)
representations are not Diophantine.

be genuinely new. We believe that it should be possible to extend both items
in the above theorem to Γ\G for Borel Anosov representations, as Mϑ = M is
compact in this case. In addition, the Diophantine condition seems technical and
could likely be removed, although we do not know how to do it.
1.5. Perspectives. We detail a non-exhaustive list of questions arising from our
work.
1.5.1. Quantum-classical correspondence. On the unit tangent bundle SN of a
convex co-compact (resp. closed) hyperbolic manifold N := Γ\Hn (rank one),
it is known that resonant states of the geodesic ﬂow associated with resonances
in a “ﬁrst band” can be integrated over the spheres of the ﬁbration SN →N
to produce resonant states (resp. eigenfunctions) for the Laplacian on N. See
[DFG15] for closed hyperbolic manifolds (or [GHW21] for general locally sym-
metric spaces of rank one), and [GHW18] for compact and convex co-compact
hyperbolic surfaces.
In higher rank, a quantum–classical correspondence was established in
[HWW23] when Γ < G is a lattice; the corresponding quantum operators form a
family of commuting elliptic operators on the locally symmetric space of higher
rank. We expect that there should also exist a higher-rank analogue of the ﬁrst
band when Γ < G is an Anosov subgroup, which would allow one to construct
resonant states for the commuting family of elliptic operators on the correspond-
ing (non-compact) quotient of the symmetric space. Note that in [DGM25] a
Quantum Classical correspondence for special classes of Anosov representations


## Page 14


14
GUEDES BONTHONNEAU, LEFEUVRE, AND WEICH
in SO(2, 2) is established. Although this is still dynamically a rank one situa-
tion (which corresponds to a spacelike geodesic ﬂow on AdS3) it is indicates that
the methods of Quantum-Classical correspondence are compatible with Anosov
subgroups.
1.5.2. Value at 0 of the Poincaré series. Order of vanishing at 0 of the Ruelle
zeta function. The value at 0 of the meromorphic extension of the Poincaré series
(1.5) should have a topological interpretation in terms of Γ and/or G.
More
precisely, it is reasonable to expect that ρ 7→ηρ(0) is locally constant in the space
of Anosov representations.
We believe that such a statement is approachable
for Hitchin representations close to the Fuchsian locus. The expected result is
ηρ(0) = 1−1/χ(Σ), where χ(Σ) = 2g −2 is the Euler characteristic of the surface
(of genus g ≥2). This should be thought of as a generalization of similar results
obtained on negatively-curved surfaces (rank 1 case), see [DR24] for instance. One
hope is be to be able to detect the connected component of the space of Anosov
representations using ηρ(0) as a topological invariant.
Similarly, the order of
vanishing of the Ruelle zeta function at zero could be a topological invariant, see
[DZ17, DGRS20, CDDP22] among other references.
1.5.3. Essential spectral gap. Given ϕ ∈a∗such that ϕ > 0 on the limit cone L ,
one may look at
Nϕ(T) := ♯{γ ∈Γ : ϕ(λ(γ)) ≤T}.
It is known that this number satisﬁes Nϕ(T) ∼T→+∞eδ(ϕ)T /δ(ϕ)T, see [Sam14,
PS17]. We emphasize that this result also follows from the meromorphic extension
of the zeta function (Theorem 1.8), using a standard Tauberian type argument
called the Wiener-Ikehara theorem (see [PP90] for instance). However, it would
be interesting to obtain an exponentially small error term in this estimate. Partial
results were already obtained in [CS23, Theorem 1.9] and [DMS24, Theorem E].
Following our spectral approach, this amounts to showing an essential spectral
gap for the critical hypersurface C(ds) + ia∗
ϑ, namely that the remainder of the
spectrum σRS lies at a positive, uniform distance away from C(ds) + ia∗
ϑ. This
essential spectral gap would also likely imply that the asymptotic expansion (1.6)
of the correlation function in inverse powers of time (Theorem 1.10, item (ii))
holds independently of the Diophantine condition, which we believe to be true.
More generally, one may consider ϕ1, ..., ϕd ∈a∗, d linear forms in the
interior of the dual of the Benoist cone (ϕi > 0 on the limit cone L ). A natural

Nϕ1,...,ϕd(T) := ♯{γ ∈Γ : ϕ1(λ(γ)), ..., ϕd(λ(γ)) ≤T}.
Likewise, this should behave as N(T) ∼T→+∞c · eλT /T α, for some constants
c, λ, α > 0, and one may also ask whether a power-saving error term is possible
to obtain.

1.5.5. Other perspectives. We now list several related questions which may:
• Relatively Anosov representations consist of a slight relaxation of the
usual Anosov subgroup assumption (Deﬁnition 1.1). In Part 2, we con-
struct a domain of discontinuity for such subgroups; however, it remains
unclear whether the spectral approach developed in the present paper can
be extended to this setting. To some extent, this situation is analogous to


## Page 16


16
GUEDES BONTHONNEAU, LEFEUVRE, AND WEICH
deﬁning Pollicott–Ruelle resonances on negatively curved manifolds with
cusps, as carried out in [BW22].
• For Hitchin representations into SL3(R), it is known that they can be in-
terpreted as the holonomy representations of convex real projective struc-
tures (see [CG97]).
This interpretation provides additional geometric
structure, which could allow one to reinterpret the resonance spectrum
we deﬁne in terms of objects intrinsic to the surface itself.
• We will see in Proposition D.2 that almost every Hitchin representation
ρ : π1(Σ) →SLn(R) is Diophantine, provided 2g ≥n3, where g ≥2 is
the genus of the closed surface Σ. This is likely to be true without any
restriction on the pair (g, n). More generally, when Γ is word hyperbolic
and G is a noncompact semisimple Lie group, most representations ρ :
Γ →G should be Diophantine. This is left for future investigation.
1.6. Strategy of proof. The article is organized as follows: we ﬁrst develop in
Part 1 the general spectral theory of free Abelian cocycles over hyperbolic ﬂows,
and then show in Part 2 that Anosov representations ﬁt into the framework
developed Part 1. At the beginning of each part, we provide a more detailed
outline of its content.

However, Theorem 1.9 is more diﬃcult and requires new technical ingre-
dients, which are ultimately based on the fact that the product resolvent (1.9)
admits a meromorphic continuation. The idea of proof is inspired by [DR24] and
consists in interpreting the Poincaré series as a distributional pairing involving
the product resolvent (1.9).
1.7. Comparison with earlier work.
1.7.1. Free Abelian cocycles. The study of ergodic properties of free Abelian co-
cycles over hyperbolic ﬂows, such as counting multidimensional periodic data for
instance (what will be referred to, in the rest of this paper, as the Lyapunov
spectrum), goes back to the pioneering work of Lalley [Lal87, Lal89b, Lal89a],
see also the work of Babillot-Ledrappier [BL98]. This topic is intimately con-
nected to the counting of periodic orbits for hyperbolic ﬂows under homological
constraints, see [KS86, PS87, Pol91, Sha92, Sha93, Ana00, PS01] among other
references.
However, all these articles make use of symbolic codings (Markov
partitions) and exploit the fact that the ﬂow is conjugate to a subshift of ﬁnite
type. Our approach to free Abelian cocycles is completely diﬀerent, and builds
on modern tools from microlocal analysis, see §1.7.4. In particular, this allows us
to recover known results and also to establish new results which are out of reach
of classical tools, such as the meromorphic extension of Ruelle zeta functions for
instance.


## Page 19


THE SPECTRUM OF ANOSOV REPRESENTATIONS
19
1.7.2. Dynamics on the trapped set. That the Aϑ-action on the trapped set J
(see (1.2)) factors as a free Abelian cocycle over a metric Anosov ﬂow was ini-
tially observed by Sambarino [Sam14, Sam15] (see also [Car23, Sam24]) who
used it to apply the tools of thermodynamic formalism within the framework of
Anosov representations. Mixing on the trapped set with respect to measures of
maximal entropy (or Patterson-Sullivan measures) was initially considering by
Thirion [Thi09] in the context of Schottky subgroups of SLn(R), by Sambarino
[Sam15] in full generality, and Chow-Sarkar [CS23]. However, these results are
not quantitative and only provide the ﬁrst term in the expansion of the corre-
lation function (1.6).
Finally, the more recent article [CLMT26] shows a full
asymptotic expansion of the correlation function on Abelian covers of isometric
extensions of Anosov ﬂows.
1.7.3. Domain of discontinuity.
Spectral theory of smooth Axiom A ﬂows
for Anosov representations. Our work builds on essential ideas from [DMS24,
DMS25] concerning the construction of domains of discontinuity for Anosov sub-
groups. A key insight of these works is that these domains give rise to smooth
hyperbolic dynamics, which can be viewed either as hyperbolic higher-rank ac-
tions—a viewpoint elaborated in the present paper—or as families of Axiom A
ﬂows, which is the perspective adopted in [DMS24, DMS25]. Most parts of The-
orem 11.12 are established in [DMS25].
However, since the primary focus of
[DMS24, DMS25] is on Axiom A ﬂows rather than on higher-rank actions (al-
though the latter are mentioned), we could not base our microlocal work in Part 1
entirely on their results. In particular, Assumption (A2) for the Abelian cocycle
over hyperbolic ﬂows is not addressed there (note, however, that a very similar
assumption appears in [DGM25, Section 5] in a related special case of space-like
geodesic ﬂows on AdS3).
We decided to give a self-contained proof of Theorem 11.12 in §11 for the
following reasons. First, while many of the results in §11 were already shown in
[DMS25], our techniques and language diﬀer, so this provides an alternative ap-
proach to their important theorems. While [DMS25] largely avoids the structure
theory of semisimple Lie groups, our approach instead fully exploits it. Second,
the (sometimes quite advanced) structure theory of reductive groups introduced
in §8–§10 is in required anyway for the meromorphic continuation of the Poincaré
series in §12. Furthermore, we also need to refer to the internal mechanisms of the
proof of the domain of discontinuity for the analysis of non-transversal points of
intersection in the proof of the meromorphic continuation of the Poincaré series.
While it should, in principle be possible to rely instead on the corresponding


## Page 20


20
GUEDES BONTHONNEAU, LEFEUVRE, AND WEICH
arguments in [DMS25], doing so would deﬁnitely make the exposition hard to
follow due to the diﬀerences in language and techniques.
Finally, our approach naturally shows that most properties of domains of
discontinuity continue to hold under weaker assumptions on Γ, such as Γ being
a divergence group or a transverse subgroup of a regular subgroup (we refer to
§10 and §11 for details) and aﬃrmatively answers a question raised in [DMS25,
§1.4]. This result could prove particularly valuable for extending the spectral
theory beyond Anosov subgroups, for instance to the class of relative Anosov
subgroups, for which we verify that all assumptions hold except the compactness
of the trapped set.
Apart from merely constructing the domains of discontinuity and the hyper-
bolic dynamical systems, [DMS24, DMS25] also derive important consequences
for the spectral theory, dynamical properties of the ﬂow and related zeta func-
tions. In [DMS24], in the setting of projective Anosov representations, dynamical
resonances for the associated Axiom A ﬂow are deﬁned. Combined with results
of Stoyanov, this provides the ﬁrst proof of exponential mixing for Sambarino’s
refraction ﬂow in this setting. In [DMS24, Theorem E], a meromorphic extension
of a family of zeta functions for general Anosov subgroups is shown; in [DGM25],
Delarue, Guillarmou and Monclair prove a meromorphic continuation of Poincaré
series associated with the space-like geodesic ﬂow on quotients of AdS3. The main
diﬀerence is that their approach reduces the higher-rank dynamics to a rank-one
dynamical system (a ﬂow), whereas our aim in the present paper is to study
the full higher-rank action and to associate to it joint resonances, multivariate
meromorphic zeta functions, and Poincaré series.
1.7.4. Spectral theory of ﬂows/actions using microlocal analysis. The theory de-
veloped in Part 1 builds on earlier work [FRS08, FS11, FT13, DZ16, DG16]
in rank 1, which establishes the meromorphic extension of the resolvent on
anisotropic spaces for hyperbolic dynamical systems using microlocal analysis.
See also [Liv04, GL06, BT07] for a similar approach based on anisotropic Banach
spaces. In turn, these techniques can be exploited to establish the meromorphic
extension of the Ruelle zeta function; see [GLP13a, DG16, DZ17, DG18].
For Anosov actions on compact manifolds (i.e. lattices in semisimple Lie
groups), a microlocal theory was recently developed in [BGHW25, GBGW24] to
study SRB measures and measures of maximal entropy [Hum25b]. The approach
developed in Part 1 of the present paper is, to a certain extent, reminiscent of
these works; see in particular §4.7. However, in the setting of lattices, the spec-
trum—also deﬁned in Crank(G)—consists of isolated points, whereas for Anosov


## Page 21


THE SPECTRUM OF ANOSOV REPRESENTATIONS
21
representations one obtains complex codimension-1 subvarieties. This is a notable
feature of convex co-compact quotients as opposed to lattices.

GUEDES BONTHONNEAU, LEFEUVRE, AND WEICH
Part 1. Free Abelian cocycles over hyperbolic dynamics
This part is organized as follows
• In §2, we introduce the notion of Abelian cocycles over hyperbolic ﬂows,
and review some of their basic properties;
• In §3, the main results of this ﬁrst part are stated, such as the existence
of the resonant spectrum, the meromorphic extension of the Ruelle zeta
function, or the meromorphic extension of the product resolvent;
• In §4, we construct the resonance spectrum and prove the meromorphic
extension of dynamical zeta functions. We also show that it corresponds
to the Taylor spectrum of a family of commuting operators;
• In §5, we study the leading resonant hypersurface and the associated
invariant measures (of maximal entropy);
• In §6, we discuss the decay of correlation for the ﬂow (Φt)t∈R with respect
to the measures of maximal entropy;
• In §7, we prove the meromorphic extension of the product resolvent.
2. Definition and first properties
In this preliminary section, we introduce the notion of free Abelian cocycles
over ﬂows and explain their relationship with locally free Abelian actions on Rk-
principal bundles; see §2.1. A key feature of free Abelian cocycles is that they may
be interpreted as reparametrizations of a single ﬂow, a point of view developed in
§2.2. We then restrict our attention to the case of interest in which the base ﬂow
is hyperbolic; this is the subject of §2.3. The notion of the limit cone, together
with related concepts, is discussed in §2.4. Finally, we conclude this section with
a discussion of admissible bundles and associated distributions in §2.5.
2.1. Deﬁnition. Let M be a smooth connected dM-dimensional manifold with-
out boundary. Let A = Rk+1, and
τ : A × M →M,
be a locally free Abelian action, mapping elements of A to diﬀeomorphisms of
M. We denote by a the Lie algebra of A, and identify it with an algebra of vector
ﬁelds on M. This inﬁnitesimal representation is denoted by
a →C∞(M, TM),
a 7→X(a) := ∂tτeta|t=0.
We let E0,M := a ⊂TM. We will make the following assumption:

2.3. Hyperbolic base dynamics. We now further investigate the properties of
the free Abelian cocycles when the ﬂow (φt)t∈R on the base is hyperbolic.
2.3.1. Free Abelian cocycles over hyperbolic ﬂows. We shall now make further
assumptions on the ﬂow (φt)t∈R on the base (Note that similar assumptions are
used in [DGM25, Section 5]).
(A2) Uniform properness. There exists two φt-invariant closed subsets Γ± ⊂
N such that the following holds: for all compact subset C∓⊂N \ Γ∓,
the map
φ : C∓× R± →N ,
(x, t) 7→φt(x)
is proper. In addition, there exists a relatively compact open subset V ⊂
N such that for all x ∈Γ±, there exists T ≥0 such that for all t ≥T,
φ∓tx ∈V .


## Page 31


THE SPECTRUM OF ANOSOV REPRESENTATIONS
31
We shall refer to these properties as uniform properness in the future (on N \Γ−)
or in the past (on N \Γ+). The sets Γ± are called the incoming (−) and outgoing
(+) tails. Assumption (A2) guarantees that points either escape to inﬁnity in the
future, or they are trapped in a compact region of N (and the same holds in the
past).
(A3) Hyperbolicity. The trapped set K := Γ−∩Γ+ is non-empty, compact,
and there exists a continuous ﬂow-invariant splitting of TN over K as
TK N = E0,N ⊕Es,N ⊕Eu,N,
where E0,N is spanned by the ﬂow direction, Es,N and Eu,N satisfy
(2.9)
∥dφt|Es,N ∥≤Ce−λt,
∥dφt|Eu,N∥≤Ce−λt,
∀t ≥0,
for some uniform constants C, λ > 0 that depend on an arbitrary choice
of an auxiliary norm ∥• ∥on TN .
It follows from (A2) −(A3) that, if K ̸= N , any relatively compact open subset
K ⋐V ⊂N , satisﬁes K = ∩t∈Rφt(V ). Such a set K is called locally maximal
in the literature. In addition, one veriﬁes that, under the above assumptions, for
all x ∈Γ±, d(φ∓tx, K ) →t→+∞0.
When K = N and N is compact, the ﬂow is said to be Anosov. When
K ̸= N , the subset K is usually very fractal. We let
(2.10)
J := K × h ⊂M.
Notice that J is A-invariant. Although not compact, it will play the role of
the “trapped set” for the whole A-action and we will loosely refer to J as the
trapped set. We also deﬁne
(2.11)
Γ±,M := Γ± × h.
To avoid confusion, we may also denote by Γ±,N := Γ± the incoming and outgoing
tails on the base N .
It is a standard fact that Es,N (resp. Eu,N) admits a natural ﬂow-invariant
extension to the incoming tail Γ−(resp. outgoing tail Γ+), see [DG16, Lemma
2.10] for instance. In addition, these bundles can be continuously extended to N
(but they are a priori not ﬂow-invariant outside of Γ±).
This leads to the following deﬁnition:

Deﬁnition 2.6 (Free Abelian cocycles over hyperbolic ﬂows). Let (M, A, τ) be
a free Abelian cocycle over a ﬂow (Deﬁnition 2.1). If the assumptions (A1), (A2)
and (A3) are satisﬁed for some choice of hyperplane h ⊂a, we say that the action
of A on M is a free Abelian cocycle over a hyperbolic ﬂow.
Alternatively, we will also call it an action of type (k, 1) over a hyperbolic
ﬂow.
Remark 2.7. More generally, actions of type (k, ℓ) could be deﬁned by requiring
that Rk+ℓacts on M in a locally free manner, that there exists a plane h ≃Rk
such that H := exp(h) acts freely and properly on M, and that there is an Anosov
Rℓaction on the quotient space N := M/H. Most of the spectral analysis carried
out in this ﬁrst part should be transferable to such actions of type (k, ℓ) and one
should get a resonance spectrum of codimension ℓvarieties in a∗
C ∼= Ck+ℓ. Note
that (0, ℓ) actions with N compact is the case of usual rank-ℓAnosov actions
and it was shown in [BGHW25] that the resonance spectrum is discrete (i.e. a
complex codimension ℓsubvariety in Cℓ).
When describing the leading resonant hypersurface of the A-action, it will
be convenient to make the following additional assumption on the dynamics over
the trapped set:
(A4) The ﬂow (φt)t∈R is topologically transitive on K .
In particular, Assumption (A4) excludes pathological path-disconnected trapped
sets.

2.4.1. Deﬁnition. Recall that, in the identiﬁcation M ≃N × h, the ﬂow (Φt)t∈R

It follows from (2.3) and (A1) −(A3) that the set L of elements a ∈a such
that there exists z ∈M such that τ(ea)z = z is discrete and splits into two

(2.17)

The hyperplane h divides a into two disjoint open half-space; in the above union,

Deﬁnition 2.8 (Limit cone of the free Abelian cocycle). The limit cone L ⊂a
of a free Abelian cocycle over a hyperbolic ﬂow is the asymptotic cone of L+
deﬁned as

L := {tλ(γ) : t > 0, γ ∈Γ} = R+L+.
2.4.2. Description of the limit cone. We ﬁrst establish its convexity:
Lemma 2.9 (Convexity of the limit cone). Assume (A1) −(A4) hold. Then the
limit cone L is convex, that is for v1, v2 ∈L , v1 + v2 ∈L .
Proof. Let γ1, γ2 ∈Γ and x1 ∈γ1, x2 ∈γ2. Let S+ ⊂K be a segment of orbit

ε > 0 is chosen small enough (such a segment exists by transitivity of (φt)t∈R on
K (Assumption (A4))). Similarly, let S−⊂K be a segment of orbit connecting
(a neighborhood of) x2 to x1. Let n ≥1. The pseudo-orbit γn
1 ⊔S+ ⊔γn
2 ⊔S−
starting at x1 ends close to x1 and can thus be closed up by the shadowing lemma,
that is there exists a genuine periodic orbit γn ∈Γ shadowing this pseudo-orbit.
In addition, a quick computation shows that

2.4.3. Further properties of the limit cone. We can now introduce a notion of
rank for the cocycle:
Deﬁnition 2.11 (Rank of the cocycle). The rank of (Φt)t∈R is deﬁned as the
minimal dimension 0 ≤d ≤k such that there exists a d+1-dimensional subspace
V ⊂a with L ⊂V . We say that the cocycle has full rank if d = k.
Remark 2.12. Note that in the setting of discrete subgroups, Benoist [Ben97]
showed that for a Zariski dense Γ < G the limit cone has non empty interior.
The full rank condition should thus be seen as an analogue of the Zariski dense
condition for discrete subgroups.
As the limit cone is (up to the choice of a positive direction for XM) intrinsic
to the A action on M, the rank does not depend on the choice of h nor XM.
We now introduce a notion of non-arithmeticity for the Lyapunov spectrum.
Deﬁnition 2.13 (Non-arithmetic condition). We say that the cocycle is non-
arithmetic if there are no non-zero 1-forms θ ∈a∗such that for all γ ∈Γ,
θ(λ(γ)) ∈2πZ.
Equivalently, there are no lines in a such that the orthogonal projection of
L onto this line is contained in a lattice.
We now discuss a strengthening of the non-arithmetic condition. We ﬁrst
introduce the following terminology for a subset of Rk+1:
Deﬁnition 2.14 (Diophantine subsets of Rk+1). Let B ⊂Rk+1 be a ﬁnite subset.
We say that it is Diophantine if there exist C, ν > 0 such that for all ξ ∈Rk+1
such that |ξ| ≥C, there exists λ ∈B such that:
|eiξ·λ −1| ≥|ξ|−ν.
In Appendix D, it is established that Lebesgue-almost every subset of car-
dinality k + 2 in Rk+1 is Diophantine. This leads to the following condition:
Deﬁnition 2.15 (Diophantine condition). We say that the cocycle is Diophantine
if there exists a ﬁnite subset A ⊂{λ(γ) : γ ∈Γ} ⊂a of the Lyapunov spectrum
(see (2.18)) which is Diophantine in the sense of Deﬁnition 2.14.
Note that the Diophantine condition (Deﬁnition 2.15) implies the non-
arithmetic condition (Deﬁnition 2.13).

2.5. Bundles and distributions.
2.5.1. Admissible bundles. Let E →M be a smooth vector bundle. As M re-
tracts onto N , E = π∗EN for some smooth vector bundle EN →N , where
π : M →N = M/H is the projection.
A section f ∈C∞(M, E) is called
a pullback section if there exists s ∈C∞(M, E) such that f = π∗s.
Consider a linear Lie algebra embedding into diﬀerential operators of order
1:
XE : a →Diﬀ1(M, E).
That is [XE(a), XE(b)] = 0 for all a, b ∈a. We say that XE is an admissible lift
of the action to E if:
• XE(a)(ϕ ⊗s) = X(a)ϕ ⊗s + ϕ ⊗XE(a)s for all a ∈a, ϕ ∈C∞(M) and
s ∈C∞(M, E);
• XE(h) (π∗s) = 0 for all s ∈C∞(N , EN), h ∈h.
In short, we say that E →M is an admissible bundle if it is equipped with such
a lift of the action. Note that the following holds:
Lemma 2.17. A section f ∈C∞(M, E) is a pullback section if and only if
XE(h)f = 0 for all h ∈h.
Proof. By assumption, any pullback section satisﬁes XE(h)π∗s = 0 so it remains
to prove the converse statement.

Conversely, we claim that any lift XEN of the vector ﬁeld XN to EN, induces

3. Spectral theory of free Abelian cocycles over hyperbolic
flows
In this section, we state our main results regarding free Abelian cocycles
over hyperbolic ﬂows. The intrinsic results are presented in §3.1 while the tech-
nical statements are deferred to §3.2.
3.1. Results I. In this ﬁrst subsection, we present our main results in a form
that is intrinsic to the A-action on M, without relying on any particular choice of
decomposition M ≃N × Rk (i.e. a choice of global trivializing section σ : N →
M). We emphasize that all objects under consideration (such as the resonance
spectrum for instance) are intrinsic to the action itself; however, our proofs will
make essential use of the existence of such a decomposition.

3.2. Results II. The results described in the previous paragraph are all intrinsic
to the action of A on M. However, they will be obtained by using the explicit
factorization of the action in terms of an Abelian cocycle over the hyperbolic ﬂow

Theorem 3.17 (Leading resonant hypersurface). For m = 0 or m = ds, the
leading resonance hypersurface C(m) is given by
C(m) = {(Pr(εJu + s′(w)), s′) : s′ ∈h∗},
where ε = 1 if m = 0, and ε = 0 if m = ds, and Pr is the pressure.
See (5.1) for the deﬁnition of the pressure. When Ju is constant, the crit-
ical hypersurface for functions is a translate of the critical hypersurface on ds-
forms, that is C(0) = C(ds) + JuαM, where αM(XM) = 1, αM(h) = 0. This is
what happens in algebraic settings such as Anosov representations. In addition,
Pr(s′w) > 0 for all s′ ∈h∗, see (5.13).
4. Construction of the spectrum
For simplicity, we construct the spectrum in the case where E = M × C is
the trivial line bundle. The adaptation to arbitrary admissible bundles is straight-
forward, up to the expected notational changes. An important observation is
that, in order to guarantee the convergence of all relevant objects (resolvents,
zeta functions, etc.) in a certain domain of a∗
C, the propagator (etXM)t∈R needs
to be exponentially bounded on certain functional spaces, which is guaranteed
by Lemma 2.20.
To improve readability, we ﬁrst treat the case N = K , i.e. when the ﬂow
is Anosov.
All of the main ideas already appear in this setting, without the
additional technical diﬃculties arising from a fractal trapped set. In the general
case, the proof proceeds through a series of reductions, ultimately leading us to
work on a closed manifold N that contains the trapped set K .
4.1. Anosov case. In this paragraph, we further assume that the ﬂow is Anosov,
i.e. K = N . For consistency with the general case, we write N := N (this is
a closed manifold).
The construction of the spectrum relies on the theory of
anisotropic Sobolev spaces which was developed in a series of work over the past
twenty years, see §1.7.4 for further bibliographical references. Throughout, we
will use [Lef25, Chapter 9] as a convenient reference.

4.2. Extension trick. To treat the case where the ﬂow is Axiom A, we ﬁrst aim
to reduce to the case where the ﬂow admits a strictly convex neighborhood; this
will allow us to leverage the analysis from [CGL24, §2.1.2].
Lemma 4.2. There exists two relatively compact neighborhoods K ⋐V0 ⋐V ′
such that for all x ∈V0, for all T ≥0:
ϕT(x) ∈V0 =⇒ϕt(x) ∈V ′, ∀t ∈[0, T],
ϕ−T(x) ∈V0 =⇒ϕt(x) ∈V ′, ∀t ∈[−T, 0].
These neighborhoods can be chosen arbitrarily small around K .
Proof. We ﬁx an arbitrary small neighborhood V ′ of K . We argue by contradic-
tion and assume that there is no such neighborhood V0 of K . We only deal with
the claim in positive times, the negative times follow from a mere adaptation of
the argument. Then, there is a nested sequence (Vn)n≥0 such that ∩n≥0Vn = K ,
xn ∈Vn, Tn ≥0 such that ϕTnxn ∈Vn, and tn ∈(0, Tn) such that ϕtnxn /∈V ′.
Notice that, up to changing tn, we can always assume that yn := ϕtnxn ∈H \ V ′,
where H is compact neighborhood of V ′.
First, observe that tn →+∞(hence Tn →+∞). Indeed, if this is bounded,
up to a subsequence, tn →t∞and similarly xn →x∞∈K . Then yn = ϕtnxn →
ϕt∞x∞=: y∞∈K , which is absurd since y∞∈H \ V ′.

4.7. Fredholm complex. Finally, we conclude this section by showing that the
resonance spectrum deﬁned above is a Taylor spectrum [Tay70a, Tay70b] for a
family of (k + 1)-commuting operators. This was established in [BGHW25] for
Anosov actions on closed manifolds. As before, we argue in the case where E is
the trivial line bundle; the extension to other vector bundles is straightforward.

To prove (6.20), we claim that it suﬃces to adapt the arguments in [Dol98].
Let B := {γ1, ..., γM} be the subset of Γ satisfying the Diophantine condition.
In the notations of [Dol98], we replace τ by a vector-valued function τ : Σ →
a = Rk+1 and b by a vector-valued spectral parameter b ∈Rk+1, where Σ is
a Markov partition. In our case, τ = (τ, W), where τ is the return time to the
Markov partition, and W : Σ →Rk is the function obtained from w by integration
along ﬂowlines between two consecutive return times to the Markov partition; the
spectral parameter is b = (b, s′), where b is the Fourier parameter corresponding
to the time direction (as in [Dol98]) and s′ is the Fourier parameter stemming
from the above decomposition.
The same estimate as [Dol98, Item (v), Theorem 1] holds under the Dio-
phantine condition, and implies (6.20) by repeating the argument in [Dol98, Sec-
tion 10]. Indeed, arguing as in [Dol98, Sections 8 and 13], if [Dol98, Item (v),
Theorem 1] does not hold, then one can construct a sequence of approximate
eigenfunctions for a family of spectral parameters |b| →+∞with approximately
constant modulus. As in [Dol98, Section 13], this results in the estimate
|ein(β,b)b·ℓi −1| ≤|b|−α,
for all 1 ≤i ≤M, where n(β, b) := [β log |b|], and ℓi is now equal to the integral
of τ along the periodic point ωi ∈Σ corresponding to an actual periodic point
xi ∈γi. Up to changing the exponent α > 0, the previous estimate may be
rewritten as
|eib′ℓi −1| ≤|b′|−α′,

## Page 117


THE SPECTRUM OF ANOSOV REPRESENTATIONS
117
Part 2. Anosov representations
In this second part, we study Anosov representations ρ : Γ →G, and prove
the theorems stated in the introduction. Here, Γ denotes a torsion-free Gromov
hyperbolic group, and G is a connected linear semisimple Lie group (see §8.1.1
and §8.4 for the precise assumptions). This includes the case of SLn(R), and
other noncompact semisimple Lie groups. Our strategy is to show that Anosov
representations naturally give rise to higher-rank dynamical systems satisfying
the assumptions of Part 1 (free Abelian cocycles over hyperbolic ﬂows).
To achieve this, we construct a domain of discontinuity for the Γ-action
on an appropriate “ﬂow space”; this idea is inspired by recent work of Delarue,
Monclair and Sanders [DMS24, DMS25]. For any P-Anosov representation ρ :
Γ →G (where P < G is a parabolic subgroup with Langlands decomposition
P = MAN), this construction yields an open A-invariant subset of ρ(Γ)\G/M
such that that the A-action on this domain factors as a free Abelian cocycle over
a hyperbolic ﬂow. Consequently, all the results established in Part 1 apply.
The part is organized as follows:
• In §8, we introduce the necessary Lie-theoretic background. In particular,
we discuss the properties of parabolic subgroups of G;
• In §9, we review some geometric aspects of the space G/M, whose homo-
geneous dynamics we study;
• In §10, we introduce the notion of divergent subgroups of G, and deﬁne
Anosov representations;
• In §11, we establish the existence of a domain of discontinuity, and show
that Anosov representations ﬁt into the theory developed in Part 1—in
particular, we prove Theorems 1.4, 1.6, 1.7, 1.8 and 1.10;
• In §12, we prove the meromorphic extension of the Poincaré series of
Anosov representations (Theorem 1.9);
• In §13, we study special features of the resonance spectrum which appear
in the context of Anosov representations.
In all our discussions, we assume that Γ is torsion-free; we will only repeat
it in main statements. For a general hyperbolic group ˜Γ containing a ﬁnite index
torsion free subgroup Γ, we could in principle study the eﬀect of moding out by
˜Γ/Γ, and the corresponding orbifolds. This is beyond our current purpose.


## Page 118


118
GUEDES BONTHONNEAU, LEFEUVRE, AND WEICH
8. Structure theory of real reductive groups
To go beyond representations valued in SLn(R), the theory of Anosov rep-
resentations relies on the structure theory of semisimple Lie groups. Formulating
precise statements and proofs requires introducing several subgroups of the am-
bient group G, many of which naturally fall into the broader class of reductive
Lie groups.
Diﬀerent viewpoints appear in the literature. In many references, notably
[GW12, GGKW17], the theory is developed using the language of algebraic
groups, while others such as [KLP17] treat G as the group of isometries of a
non-compact symmetric space. Although the underlying objects are the same,
the presentations can diﬀer substantially. For our purposes—and hopefully for
the reader’s convenience—we review the structure theory in some detail, follow-
ing the approach of Knapp [Kna96, §VII.2], which provides a convenient reference
for most of the results we need. We will assume that the target group G is real
reductive in the sense of Knapp (note that the deﬁnition varies slightly across the
literature). As explained in §8.4, this class essentially coincides with the target
groups considered, for instance, in [GGKW17].
Before turning to the general case, note that any connected, closed linear
subgroup G < GLn(R) that is stable under the Cartan involution Θ : g 7→g−⊤
is real reductive.
This class already contains most examples relevant to our
setting. However, when studying the ﬁne structure of parabolic subgroups, one
naturally encounters reductive groups that are not connected (for instance in
the Langlands decomposition). For this reason, it will be convenient to allow
possibly disconnected real reductive groups. As a guiding example, we illustrate
the notions introduced in this section in the case G = SLn(R) (see §8.6); readers
less familiar with Lie group theory may wish to keep this example in mind.
8.1. Basic Lie algebra decompositions.
8.1.1. Real reductive Lie groups. We ﬁrst recall the deﬁnition of a real reductive
Lie group taken from [Kna96, §VII.2]:
Deﬁnition 8.1 (Real reductive Lie group). A real reductive Lie group is a 4-tuple
(G, K, θ, β) consisting of a Lie group G, a maximal compact subgroup K ⊂G, an
Ad(K) invariant Lie-algebra involution θ : g →g and a non-degenerate bilinear
form β on g which is Ad(G)- and θ-invariant, such that
(8.1)
⟨•, •⟩:= −β(•, θ•)


## Page 119


THE SPECTRUM OF ANOSOV REPRESENTATIONS
119
deﬁnes a scalar product on g. Additionally, it is required that
(i) g is reductive, i.e g = [g, g] ⊕zg is the sum of its center with its derived
algebra gss := [g, g], which is semisimple.
(ii) The decomposition of g into +1 and −1 eigenspaces under θ is g = k ⊕s,
where k is the Lie algebra of K.
(iii) This decomposition is orthogonal.
(iv) The map K × s ∋(k, s) 7→k exp s ∈G is a global diﬀeomorphism.
(v) Seen as automorphisms of gC, all Ad(g), g ∈G lie in the identity compo-
nent Int(gC).
Let us also make some basic remarks (see [Kna96, §VII.2]):
• We will usually only say that G is reductive and K, θ, β are clear from
the context.
• Item (v) is naturally satisﬁed when G is connected.
• If G is a connected matrix group (real or complex) stable under inverse
adjoint, G is reductive, with K the intersection of G with the unitary
group, θ(H) = −H∗, and β is
β(X, Y ) = TrRn×n(XY ).
This shows that many classical Lie groups are actually reductive.
• Semisimple Lie groups with ﬁnite center are reductive.
The main diﬀerence between semisimple and reductive groups is the presence of
non-discrete centers. This requires some careful consideration.
• The center of a reductive group is reductive [Kna96, Corollary 7.26].
• Denote by Gss the analytic subgroup generated by exp(gss) and by Zvec =
exp(s ∩zg) (this is called the split component of G). Now set 0G = KGss.
This is a closed subgroup, reductive, with compact center. Additionally
we have the isomorphism (see [Kna96, Proposition 7.27]):
(8.2)
G = 0G × Zvec.
• Invariance of β under Ad(G) implies that zg is orthogonal to gss with
respect to ⟨•, •⟩. The restriction β|s∩zg can also be chosen arbitrarily.
• If G is reductive, and h is a θ-stable Abelian subalgebra of its Lie algebra
g, then the centralizer ZG(h) is reductive. The new K, θ, β are given by
intersection and restriction [Kna96, Proposition 7.25].

The adjoint representation provides a natural embedding ad : a →End(g)
valued in self-adjoint endomorphisms. The joint reduction of elements of a yields

(8.4)

This is actually an equality when α, β, α + β are all non zero, see Proposition
E.3, but the proof is less elementary. The roots vanish when restricted to the
center. It follows that they are exactly the trivial extension to a of the restricted
roots of (gss, a ∩gss). Since gss is semisimple, we can apply [Kna96, Proposition
6.53] and deduce that these restrictions to a ∩gss form an abstract root system
in the dual (a ∩gss)∗. This root system is possibly non-reduced. Also observe
that contrary to root spaces of g (that are always one dimensional and lie in gC),
the restricted root spaces may be of higher dimension (they are actually linear
combinations of the true root spaces).
For our purposes, the split component of G is not the most important
object, so, from now on, we will make the following standing assumption: unless
explicitly stated, G is a real reductive group with compact center. We
will come back to the case with non-trivial split center in §8.4 and §13.1.

8.4. Relation with algebraic groups and precise assumptions. The pur-
pose of this section is to explain why assuming as we will from now on that G
consists of the real points of a linear, real algebraic, Zariski connected,
semisimple group with trivial center results in no loss of generality from the
original assumption that G was real reductive with compact center. If G is such
a group, we will, in §13.1, consider the case of G × Rn. Notice that the assump-
tion in (for example) [GGKW17, §2.2] was that G consists of the real points of a
linear, real algebraic, Zariski connected, reductive group. Our assump-
tions are thus the same, except that we have decided to forget the real-algebraic
structure of the split component.
In the literature on reductive groups, there exists a divide between authors
who use the language of real Lie groups, and the others that rely on the theory of
real algebraic groups. While in general some real Lie groups are not algebraic, the
case of reductive groups is easier to understand. In our main reference [Kna96],
the author has made a signiﬁcant eﬀort to present proofs on the real Lie group
side that do not rely on algebraic arguments. We will not live up to this standard,
and will instead use some lemmata from the literature, proven for real reductive
algebraic groups. Here we establish the basis to justify this.
Let us come back to the general real reductive case, where G = 0G × Rn,
for some real reductive 0G with compact center, and n ≥0. Consider an Iwasawa
0a ⊂0g, a corresponding system of roots and a generalized Weyl chamber 0C ⊂
0a. Then MC = 0ZG(C ) = M0C ⊂0G. The object of our attention will be the
right action of C = 0C ⊕Rn and the left action of a discrete subgroup Γ ⊂G on
G/MC = 0G/MC × Rn.
Notice that since 0G has a compact center, ker Ad0G ⊂Z0G(0a) ⊂MC . In
particular, the action on the left and on the right on the factor 0G/MC factors
through Ad, so that we may and will replace 0G by Ad(0G) = Ad(G) and G by
Ad(G)×Rn without loss of generality. Recall from [Kna96, Proposition 6.30] that
Ad(G) has a trivial center, so that all reductive groups H ⊂Ad(G) introduced
in the previous paragraphs lie in the Harish-Chandra class, i.e., satisfy item
(vi) Hss is closed and has ﬁnite center
(see p. 447, Proposition 7.20 and the following remark from [Kna96]).
Recall from [Hum81] that the real points G(R) of a real-algebraic linear
group G are the matrices in GL(V ) that solve some collection of polynomial
equations, for V a real vector space. The complex points G(C) are the solutions
of the same equations in GL(V ⊗C), i.e with complex coeﬃcients. As a real
variety, G may or may not be irreducible. The (algebraic) component of identity


## Page 128


128
GUEDES BONTHONNEAU, LEFEUVRE, AND WEICH
G0 of G is the (only) irreducible component of G (as a real variety) that contains
the identity (see [Hum81, §7.3]). Note that when G0 is connected in the Zariski
topology, G0(C) is always connected in the real topology sense, while G0(R) may
or may not be connected (but has a ﬁnite number of components). It is customary
in the theory of algebraic groups to say that G is connected if G = G0, to avoid
the word irreducible, which has another meaning in the context of representations.
We will write Zariski connected to avoid confusion.
Proposition 8.9. Let G be a real reductive group. There exists a linear real
algebraic semisimple Zariski connected Lie group G so that
Ad(G) ⊂G(R).
The image is a union of connected components. In particular
G0/Z(G0) ≃G(R)0.
Note that ker Ad ∩0G ⊂K.
Item (v) of Deﬁnition 8.1 is crucial here.
Proof. We consider the image Ad(G) in the automorphism group of gss. The
automorphism group of a real Lie algebra is by deﬁnition linear and always a real
algebraic group; Aut(gss) is no exception. It is, however, in general not connected.
Denote by G = Int(gss) its component of identity. Item (v) in Deﬁnition 8.1
implies that Ad takes values in G(R). However, since gss is semisimple, we get
Lie(G) = g. Since Ad(G) is a subgroup with the same Lie algebra, it must be a
union of connected components of G(R).
The statement on the connected component of identity follows from the
observation that ker Ad ∩G0 = Z(G) ∩G0, and the fact that [Kna96, Corollary
7.26(a)] implies that Z(G) ∩G0 = Z(G0).
Finally, to see that ker Ad∩0G ⊂K, it suﬃces to prove that ker Ad∩0G is
compact. This follows from an elementary study of the action of Ad on restricted
root spaces.
□
We discuss an example that may help the casual reader to understand
what we are avoiding with our assumptions. For integers p, q > 0, consider the
orthogonal group O(p, q). Since the determinant can take values +1 or −1 on
O(p, q)(C), O(p, q) is not Zariski connected, and SO(p, q) = O(p, q)0.
(1) If p + q is odd, −1 ∈O(p, q) has determinant −1 and lies in the kernel
of Ad. For this reason, we can consider representations taking values in
O(p, q) if p + q is odd (because we can mod out ±1).

is valued in SO(p, q).
Dealing with the truly O(p, q)-valued representations, and in general in Aut(gss),
would require a careful algebraic investigation, which, while interesting, is beyond
our purpose in this article.

8.5.4. Jordan projection and decomposition. The Jordan decomposition is a fea-
ture of Zariski connected algebraic groups. Elements of G that are conjugate to
an element of K (resp. A+, N) are called elliptic (resp. hyperbolic, unipotent).
A Jordan decomposition of g is a commuting product
g = gellghypguni,
where gell (resp. ghyp, guni) is elliptic (resp. hyperbolic, unipotent). If it is possible
to ﬁnd a single γ so that Adγgell ∈K, Adγghyp ∈A+, Adγguni ∈N, we say that
the decomposition is simultaneously conjugated. The following is classical (see
[GGKW17, §2.4])
Proposition 8.14. Every g ∈G admits a unique Jordan decomposition; it is
simultaneously conjugated. Correspondingly, writing

9. Geometry of the phase space
Let C be a generalized Weyl chamber as in the previous section.
The
purpose of this section is to review the structure of G/MC . This is the space on
which our higher rank dynamical system acts via the right action of AC; we call
G/MC the phase space of this dynamics. We start by decomposing G/MC in terms
of the partial ﬂag spaces G/P±C by introducing generalized Hopf coordinates. We
then investigate the (diﬀerential) geometry of the phase space.
9.1. Flag spaces. Generalized Hopf coordinates. As mentioned in §8.2.4,
every parabolic subgroup is self-normalizing, so that G/PC parametrizes the set
of parabolic subgroups conjugate to PC . The space G/PC is called the space of
partial ﬂags; when C = a++ is an open Weyl chamber, it is called the complete
ﬂag space, a terminology inspired by the case G = SLn(R), see Example 9.6.
These spaces will play a central role in the sequel.

10. Divergent, regular, transverse and Anosov subgroups
Throughout this section, we ﬁx some generalized Weyl chamber C , and
review the concepts of divergent, regular, transverse and Anosov subgroups. The
notions of divergent, regular and transverse subgroups were introduced over the
years to deepen the understanding of Anosov subgroups. For an account of the
terminology, see the recent survey [CZZ25].
10.1. C -divergence and limit points. The notion of C -divergence will be cru-
cial in establishing the existence of a domain of discontinuity. In this paragraph,
we consider sequences of elements in G. The concepts below were originally de-
veloped by Benoist, see [Ben97, §3]. The purpose is to ﬁnd for general sequences
of elements of G a notion that replicates proximality for sequences of the form
(gℓ)ℓ≥1.

10.2. Divergent subgroups. We are now in position to introduce the notion of
C -divergent subgroups of G. Given a sequence (γℓ)ℓ≥0, we write γℓ→∞if for all
compact subset Q ⊂G, there exists ℓ(Q) ≥0 such that γℓ/∈Q for all ℓ≥ℓ(Q).
10.2.1. Deﬁnition. We start with the deﬁnition and then discuss the main objects
associated with such divergent subgroups.
Deﬁnition 10.13 (C -divergent subgroups). A discrete subgroup Γ < G is called
C -divergent if for all sequences (γℓ)ℓ≥0 ⊂Γ such that γℓ→∞, one has γℓ→C ∞.
That is divergent sequences in Γ are C -divergent.
Let a++ ≥C be an open Weyl chamber.
Recall that C ◦= conv(C ∪
ιa++(C )) is the smallest ιa++-invariant Weyl chamber. The following lemma as-
serts that when Γ is a subgroup, C -divergence implies C ◦-divergence.
Lemma 10.14. If Γ < G is a C -divergent group, then
(i) For any w ∈W, Γ is wC divergent
(ii) Γ is −C divergent
(iii) Γ is C ◦-divergent.
Remark 10.15. It follows from this lemma that, up to enlarging C , it is always
possible to ensure that Γ is C -divergent for a ιa++-invariant Weyl chamber C .
Proof. (i) By Lemma 10.3 we see that C -divergence implies wC -divergence. (ii)
Since Γ < G is a group, for any divergent sequence (γℓ)ℓ≥0, (γ−1
ℓ)ℓ≥0 is also C -
divergent and thus by Lemma 10.3 we ﬁnd that Γ is −C -divergent. (iii) Hence,
by (1) and (2), (γℓ)ℓ≥0 is divergent with respect to the roots in ∆+
ι(C ) ∪∆+

C ◦,
that is it is C ◦-divergent.
□

10.3. Transverse subgroups. We now discuss the notion of transversality for
the limit set.
Deﬁnition 10.23 (C -transverse subgroups). Let Γ < G be a discrete group, C
a generalized Weyl chamber and a++ ≥C an open Weyl chamber.
(i) If C is ιa++-invariant, we call Γ a C -transverse group if Γ is C -divergent
and if for all ξ1, ξ2 ∈ΛC with ξ1 ̸= ξ2 we have ξ1 ⋔IC (ξ2).
(ii) More generally, if C is not ι-invariant, we say that Γ is C -transverse if it
is C ◦-transverse.
A direct consequence of transversality is that for transverse groups Γ ⊂G
and ι invariant faces,

10.4. Anosov subgroups and representations.
10.4.1. Deﬁnition. Given a discrete and ﬁnitely generated subgroup Γ < G, it is
a standard tool to consider the word length of the generating set | • | on Γ. In
order that this word length deﬁnes a metric d(γ1, γ2) = |γ−1
1 γ2|, we need that the
generating set is closed under inversion and thus |γ−1| = |γ|. The metric spaces
obtained in this way are, up to quasi-isometry, independent of the choice of the
generating set (see e.g. [BH99, I.8.17]).
Deﬁnition 10.25 (PC -Anosov subgroups). Let Γ < G be a discrete subgroup.
Let C ⊂a be a generalized Weyl chamber. The subgroup is PC-Anosov if it is a
C -divergent subgroup (in the sense of Deﬁnition 10.13) and there exist C1, C2 > 0
such that:
α(µ(γ)) ≥C1|γ| −C2,
∀γ ∈Γ, α ∈∆+
C.
In other words, Anosov subgroups are C -divergent subgroups with a quanti-
tative divergence condition. As this condition is invariant under quasi-isometries
on Γ, this condition is independent of the choice of metric | • | by the above
remark.
Note that by [KLP18, Theorem 1.4], [BPS19, Theorem 3.2], the Anosov
property as introduced here implies that Γ is in fact Gromov-hyperbolic.

10.4.3. Anosov representations. Anosov representations of word hyperbolic
groups are often studied in place of Anosov subgroups. This distinction turns
out to be useful when varying the representation, and considering representa-
tions in families.
Let Γ be a Gromov hyperbolic group, and let ρ : Γ →G be a discrete and
faithful representation. Then ρ is a PC -Anosov representation if ρ(Γ) < G is a
PC -Anosov subgroup in the above sense (Deﬁnition 10.25). A typical example of
a word-hyperbolic group is provided by Γ = π1(Σ), a surface group, where Σ is a
closed topological orientable surface of genus ≥2.

See [GGKW17, Theorem 5.3 (4)] for instance.
11. Domains of discontinuity
In this section, we show that for C -divergent subgroups there exists an AC -
invariant domain of discontinuity in G/MC . Under the additional assumption of
C -transversality—and, more strongly, the C -Anosov property—the dynamics of
AC become progressively better behaved. In particular, one can ultimately show
that they satisfy all the assumptions of Part 1 (see Theorem 11.12 below). Such
domains were ﬁrst constructed by Delarue, Monclair and Sanders in [DMS24]
for projective Anosov groups and later extended to general Anosov subgroups in
[DMS25]. We provide a self-contained proof of the existence and key properties
of these DMS domains, together with additional results relevant to our spectral
study of higher-rank actions. Moreover, our techniques and arguments diﬀer in
ﬂavour from those in [DMS24, DMS25].
11.1. Deﬁnition. As above, C is a ﬁxed generalized Weyl chamber and Γ < G
is a discrete, C -divergent subgroup of G. Recall that the Hopf coordinates (see
(9.4)) provide a diﬀeomorphism
G/MC ≃G/PC
⋔× G/P−C × aC.
We can now introduce the following domain of discontinuity in G/MC, ﬁrst con-
structed by Delarue, Monclair and Sanders [DMS24, DMS25]:
Deﬁnition 11.1 (DMS domain). Let Γ < G be a discrete and torsion-free C -
divergent subgroup. The DMS domain associated with Γ is deﬁned as:
ΩC

11.4. Hyperbolicity. In §9.2 we introduced G-invariant, AC -invariant subbun-
dles of T(G/MC), see (9.11). They descend naturally to M and N and are the
natural candidates for stable/unstable bundles. However, since MC is noncom-
pact there is in general no G-invariant Riemannian metric on G/MC. We have
to content ourselves with an arbitrary analytic Riemannian metric on N . The
proof that the above mentioned bundles are (un)stable bundles is quite subtle
and needs the assumption of C -regularity (see Deﬁnition 10.21), which we will
make from now on.

is a nonnegative distribution, it implies that mu|Sx0Σ = 0, and by ﬂow-invariance,
mu ≡0 in restriction to the ﬂow-out of Sx0Σ. However, the latter being dense in
the trapped set K , and the geodesic ﬂow being transitive, one easily concludes
by a Fubini-type argument that mu ≡0 on SΣ, which is a contradiction. The
same argument can be carried out in higher rank as well, using (7.4) to express
the polar part of the inverse of the product resolvent.

We will actually be in a slightly more involved geometric situation, where

If S ⊂M is a non-orientable submanifold of dimension m, we denote by o(TS) its
bundle of orientations. Assume that o(TS) is the restriction to S of a Z2-bundle
o over M. Then we can see the total space o as a Z2-cover over M, and o(TS)
as a submanifold of o, which must be orientable and is a double cover of S. o
has a unique non-trivial deck transformation τ, that reverses the orientation of
o(TS). It also reverses the orientation of the Z2-bundle o∗o (the pullback to the
total space o of the bundle o). Since o∗o is trivial, it admits a section σ, and
τ ∗([o(TS)] × σ) = (−[o(TS)]) × (−σ) = [o(TS)] × σ.
It follows that [o(TS)]×σ is a well deﬁned distributional section of twisted forms
(Λn−mT ∗M)o which we denote [S]o. It only depends on chosing an orientation of
the double cover o and of o(TS).

12.3. Orientability. In order to express the Poincaré series as a pairing of cur-
rents, we need to discuss the orientations on G/MC and on its submanifolds
(xK)MC . We start by observing that G/MC is itself orientable. Indeed, recall
(9.10) which is an Ad(MC )-invariant decomposition.

Next we observe that there is a diﬀeomorphism
G/PC ≃K/KC ∋kKC 7→xkMC ∈(xK)MC ⊂G/MC.
The partial ﬂag manifold is not always orientable (see [PSMdSS12] for a rather
complete discussion of this subject). However recall that the unstable foliation
Eu is integrable, and its leaves are subsets of G/PC; this suggests that the lack
of orientation of xKMC is related to the orientation of Eu.
Lemma 12.9. If G/PC is non-orientable, then Eu is not orientable, and
o(Eu)|(xK)MC ≃o(T(xK)MC ).
Likewise,
o(Ews)|(xKAC)MC ≃o(T(xKAC )MC ),
and since G/MC is orientable, o(Ews) ≃o(Eu).

Lemma 12.10. We pick orientations, distinguishing two cases.
(i) If G/PC is orientable, we choose an orientation ε for G/MC , and an
orientation δ for KMC . Then we obtain a natural orientation on each
xKMC and each xKAC MC by pushforward.
(ii) If G/PC is non-orientable, we choose an orientation ε on o(Eu), and an
orientation δ on o(TKMC ). Then we obtain a natural orientation on each
o(TxKMC ) and each o(TxKAC MC ) by pushforward.

13. Further remarks
The dynamical system AC ↷MC is a very special kind of Abelian cocycle
over hyperbolic dynamics; we now make further remarks about this structure.
13.1. General reductive groups. As mentioned at the end of §8.1.1, we have
assumed so far that the group G is reductive with compact center, and the reader
may recall that general reductive groups can be decomposed as G × Rk, where G
is reductive and has compact center. Now we explain how it is possible to deal
with the presence of a non-trivial split component in the center.
Deﬁnition 13.1. Let ρ : Γ →G×Rk be a faithful homomorphism from a discrete
group. We say that ρ is Anosov provided the projection πρ : Γ →G is Anosov.
Correspondingly, we denote
ΩC
DMS(ρ) = ΩC
DMS(πρ) × Rk ⊂(G × Rk)/MC.
All Anosov representations of Γ in G × Rk are obtained by taking the
product of an Anosov representation of Γ in G, and an arbitrary representation
of Γab in Rk. In the case that Γ is the π1 of a compact surface Σ, Γab is isomorphic
to the ﬁrst singular homology H1(Σ).
Keeping the notation N = Γ\ΩC
DMS(πρ)/ ker β, it is an elementary conse-
quence of Theorem 11.12 that
Γ\ΩC
DMS(ρ) →N
is a ker β × Rk-principal bundle. Taking the projection on the Rk component, we
obtain thus a free Abelian cocycle wρ valued in Rk over the ﬂow in N .
Proposition 13.2. The Abelian cocycles obtained in this fashion take the form
α(XN), where α is a Rk-valued closed 1-form on N .

Appendix A. Triviality of principal Rk-bundles
Lemma A.1. Every Rk-principal bundle over a paracompact manifold N admits
a section (i.e. is trivial). This holds in C∞and analytic settings.
Proof. One can use the theory of classifying spaces, or directly construct a global
section for any such bundles. Let {U} be a locally ﬁnite open cover of N , suﬃ-
ciently ﬁne so that we have a system of local smooth sections {σU}. Enumerate
the cover as U0, . . . , Uℓ, . . . , and set Vℓ= U0 ∪· · · ∪Uℓ. Then we construct a
sequence σℓof sections of the bundle over Vℓ. First, set σ0 = σU0. Next, observe
that on U0 ∩U1,
σU1 −σU0 = f1,
for some smooth Rk valued function f1, that we can extend continously to U1 as
a function ˜f1, and set
σ1 =
(
σ0
on U0,
σU1 −˜f1
on U1, .
We proceed by induction, replacing at each step ℓ≥2, U0 by Vℓ−1, and U1 by Uℓ.
Since N is paracompact, for any x in N, σ(x) := σℓ(x) is deﬁned and constant for
all ℓ> ℓ(x) for some ℓ(x) < ∞. This provides us with a smooth global section σ
as announced.
In the analytic case, we have to modify the argument above. The argument
above relied on the fact that the sheaf of C∞function is ﬂabby, and its cohomology
is thus trivial. For the sheaf of real analytic functions the same argument cannot
expect to apply. However we can use a series of deep results to reach the same
conclusion.
(1) Each real analytic paracompact manifold E admits an embedding into a
complex manifold EC, where it is the ﬁxed point set of a anti-holomorphic
involution (Theorem of Whitney).
(2) Each neighbourhood of E in EC contains a neighbourhood of E that is
Stein (Theorem of Grauert).
(3) The p-cohomology of the sheaf of holomorphic sections on Stein manifolds
vanishes for p > 0 (Cartan’s Theorem B).
In the argument above, if the bundle were real analytic, we could pick our local
sections σj to be real analytic, and consider a small complex neighbourhood EC
of E, covered by open sets UC
j , so that UC
j ∩E = Uj, j ≥0, and so that each
σj extends holomorphically to UC
j . Then Cartan’s theorem B ensures that the

Appendix B. Multivariable Fredholm theory
Let E1, E2 be two Banach spaces and U ⊂Cn. We recall the following
standard terminology:
Deﬁnition B.1 (Holomorphic and meromorphic families of operators). The fam-
ily of bounded operators U ∋s 7→Q(s) ∈L(E1, E2) is holomorphic if for all
u ∈E1, v ∈E∗
2, the function U ∋s 7→(Q(s)u, v) ∈C is holomorphic.
In
turn, s 7→P(s) ∈L(E1, E2) is meromorphic if it can be written locally as
P(s) = Q(s)/r(s), for some holomorphic family s 7→Q(s) ∈L(E1, E2) and
holomorphic function r. Finally we call s 7→P(s) ﬁnitely meromorphic if it can
be written as the sum of a meromorphic familiy of operators that are at each
point ﬁnite rank operators plus a homomorphic family of operators.
We shall prove the following version of the analytic Fredholm theorem in
several variables:
Theorem B.2 (The analytic Fredholm theorem). Let U ⊂Cn be an open sub-
set, U ∋s 7→P(s) ∈L(E1, E2) be a holomorphic family of Fredholm operators
between two Banach spaces E1 and E2. Suppose that there exists s0 ∈U such that
P(s0) : E1 →E2 is invertible. Then U ∋s 7→P(s)−1 ∈L(E1, E2) is a ﬁnitely
meromorphic family of operators. The subset
S := {s ∈U : P(s) : E1 →E2 is not invertible}
is a complex codimension 1 variety, i.e. locally the 0 level set of a holomorphic
function, and s 7→P(s)−1 ∈L(E2, E1) is holomorphic on U \ S.
Notice that the invertibility assumption at s0 implies that the family of
Fredholm operators s 7→P(s) has index 0.
Proof. If E1 = E2 ≃CN are both ﬁnite-dimensional (of same dimension), the
statement is immediate since
P(s)−1 =
1
det P(s)ad(P(s)),
where ad stands for the adjugate matrix. The two maps s 7→ad(P(s)), det(P(s))
are holomorphic and S is given by the 0 level set of det(P(•)). (Notice that
det(P(•)) ̸= 0 as there exists a point s0 of invertibility.)

following result will be needed in the article:
Lemma C.1. Let U ⊂Cn be an open subset such that for every w ∈Cn−1,
Uw := U ∩C × {w} is connected and non empty. Let h : U →C be a non

Let us check that ˜h is actually holomorphic in all variables, and extends

remain in the domain of deﬁnition of h, ensuring that
˜h(z1(w), w) = h(z1(w), w).
This implies that ˜h does not depend on the choice of z0(w), and also that ˜h ≡h
on U.
To conclude that ˜h is holomorphic, observe that, since U is open, we may
always take z0(w) to be locally constant in w, and then take a path from z0 to
z that does not depend on w. It is then a simple case of diﬀerentiating under
the integral to conclude that ˜h is C1 in (z, w), and satisﬁes the Cauchy-Riemann
equations near any point (z, w) not in the singular set of h1. It thus turns out
that ˜h is holomorphic on the complement of an analytic set (the singular set
of h1). To conclude that it is globally holomorphic, it remains to show that ˜h
is locally bounded near any point of the singular set of h1 (and then apply the
removable singularity theorem in several variables).

Appendix D. Diophantine subsets and representations
D.1. Diophantine subsets in Euclidean space. Recall from Deﬁnition 2.14
that a subset A ⊂Rk is Diophantine if there exists C, ν > 0 such that for all
ξ ∈Rk such that |ξ| ≥C, there exists λ ∈A such that
(D.1)
|eiλξ −1| ≥|ξ|−ν.
More generally, given a ﬁxed exponent ν > 0, we say that A ⊂Rk is ν-
Diophantine if there exists C > 0 such that (D.1) holds for all |ξ| ≥C.
Proposition D.1. For all ν > k, the set of ν-Diophantine (k + 1)-tuples has full
measure in (Rk)k+1.
In other words,
{λ = (λ0, ..., λk) ∈(Rk)k+1 : ∃C > 0, ∀|ξ| ≥C, ∃ℓ∈{0, ..., k}, |eiξλℓ−1| ≥|ξ|−ν}
has full measure in (Rk)k+1.
Of course, one could formulate a general proposition with d-tuples instead
of (k + 1)-tuples. We emphasize that the proof fails for d ≤k.

{λ ∈(Rk)k+1 : ∃ξn →+∞, ∀ℓ= 0, . . . , k, |eiλℓξn −1| ≤|ξn|−ν}

D.2. Diophantine representations. Given a word-hyperbolic group Γ, and G
a semisimple Lie group of noncompact type, we show that, under an appropriate
condition on Γ and G, most representations ρ : Γ →G are Diophantine in
the sense that their Lyapunov spectrum satisﬁes the Diophantine condition (see
Deﬁnition 2.14). In this paragraph, we will make the standing assumption that
Γ is ﬁnitely-generated, with s ≥1 generators, and ﬁnitely-presented with r ≥0
relations. That is
Γ = {⟨γ1, ..., γs⟩: ri(γ1, ..., γs) = 1, 1 ≤i ≤r},
where ri denotes a relation (involving product and inverse operations). Conse-
quently, Hom(Γ, G) can be identiﬁed with
Hom(Γ, G) ≃{g1, ..., gs ∈Gs : Ri(g1, ..., gs) = 1, 1 ≤i ≤r},
where Ri : Gs →G are real non-constant analytic functions corresponding to
the relations ri, 1 ≤i ≤r. The space Hom(Γ, G) is a real analytic subvariety
of Gs; it is therefore a stratiﬁed space with an open dense maximal stratum
S0 ⊂Hom(Γ, G) which is an analytic submanifold of Gs (see [BM88, 2.10-2.11]).
By deﬁnition of the maximal stratum, on S0, the rank of Gs →Gr, (g1, ..., gs) 7→
(R1(g1, ..., gs), ..., Rr(g1, ..., gs)) is maximal, equal to 0 < d ≤r · dim G.
For
simplicity, we will further assume that d = r · dim G, which is the case is most
examples, and therefore dim S0 = (r−s) dim(G). The general case can be handled
by slightly adapting the argument. As S0 is an analytic submanifold, there is
a well-deﬁned Lebesgue class; it therefore makes sense to talk about Lebesgue
almost every point of S0.
Proposition D.2 (Lebesgue almost every representation is Diophantine). As-
sume that dim S0 = (s−r) dim(G) and that s ≥(r dim(G)+1)(rank(G)+1). Then
Lebesgue almost every Borel Anosov representation ρ : Γ →G is Diophantine.
The statement is empty if there are no Borel Anosov representations. A
particular case where the proposition applies is Γ = π1(Σg) and G = SLn(R),
provided 2g ≥n3. In this case Γ = {⟨a1, b1, ..., a2g, b2g⟩
:

Appendix F. The cotangent bundle of a (Riemannian) symmetric
space
Consider G a semisimple real non-compact Lie group, with maximal com-
pact K, and the associated symmetric space X = G/K. The cotangent bundle
of X is the associated bundle
T ∗X ≃TX ≃G ×K s.
Naturally, T ∗X decomposes into G-invariant subbundles, that correspond to the
orbits of Ad(K) on s. In the so-called rank one case, these orbits are concentric
spheres and {0}, but in higher rank, the maximal compact
K ⊂SO(s)
may not act transitively on the sphere, so the orbits may be smaller. Since

one ﬁnds that the space of orbits is exactly
a/W(G, A).
The orbits form thus an analytic stratiﬁcation of T ∗X. If a is in an open Weyl
chamber,
M = {k ∈K | kak−1 = a},
So the corresponding orbit is diﬀeomorphic to G/M: this is a generic orbit.
Likewise, for C some generalized Weyl chamber and a ∈C , we ﬁnd that
KC = MC ∩K = {k ∈K | kak−1 = a},
so that the corresponding orbit is of the form G/KC, on which we have a right
action of AC. The tangent space of such an orbit writes
T(G/KC) ≃G ×KC (mC ∩s ⊕aC ⊕n+
C ⊕n−
C).
The ﬁrst summand in the RHS gives rise to an integrable foliation of neutral
directions (whose leaves are diﬀeomorphic to the symmetric space MC /KC). If
Γ ⊂G is a discrete subgroup,
(F.1)
Γ\G/KC
is a legitimate manifold endowed with a AC action, but it is reasonable to expect
that some leaves of the neutral direction may be dense, or at least not closed, so
that
Γ\G/MC
may not be a Hausdorﬀspace. This justiﬁes somewhat the need for restricting
our attention to a discontinuity domain ΩC
DMS in our investigations.
Question. It would be interesting to understand whether the AC dynamics
on (F.1) can be analyzed eﬀectively as a partially hyperbolic lift of the dynamics
on our space Γ\ΩC
DMS.
To close this subsection, we recall how one can relate these A(C ) actions
with well known dynamical objects of Riemannian geometry. Here we are relying
on the excellent text [Hil05] from which we are extracting some crucial results.
Consider f : T ∗(G/K) →R a G-invariant smooth function. We can consider its


## Page 211


THE SPECTRUM OF ANOSOV REPRESENTATIONS
211
Hamilton vector ﬁeld Hf. Using the Killing form on s, we can also consider the
gradient ∇f : s →s, and ﬁnd that ∇f : a →a [Hil05, Lemma 1.5]. Then the
Hamilton ﬂow of f, denoted Φf
t writes [Hil05, eq.(1.10)]
Φf
t [g, X] = [get∇f(X), X].
In particular, this ﬂow preserves the G-orbits. If f ′ is another smooth function,
we ﬁnd that the corresponding vector ﬁelds commute [Hil05, eq.(1.11)]
[Hf, Hf′] = 0.
If a ∈a is in some open Weyl chamber, and
G · [e, a] = {[g, a] | g ∈G} ≃G/M
is the corresponding G orbit, we obtain a ﬂow on G/M given by [Hil05, eq. (1.16)]
Φf,a
t (gM) = get∇f(a)M.
We ﬁnd thus that Φf
t is just an instance of the A action on G/M. From the struc-
ture of the G-orbits on T(G/K) we presented above, we ﬁnd that the function f
is completely determined by its values on a, and that the restriction f|a must be
W(G, A)-invariant. This forces the gradient of f at a point a ∈C to lie in aC,
corresponding to the fact that for orbits associated with a point a lying on a face
C , only aC acts on the orbit.
In [Hil05, §1.3] the reader will ﬁnd an explanation that there are suﬃcient
algebraically independent W(G, A)-invariant polynomials of a so that we recover,
using only polynomial f, all the directions of the action. In the case of SL3(R),
it is quite simple. Writing
a = {(x1, x2, x3) | x1 + x2 + x3 = 0},
consider the two generators
f1 = x2
1 + x2
2 + x2
3,
f2 = x3
1 + x3
2 + x3
3.
They are invariant under permutation of indices (i.e the action of W(G, A) in
this context), and are independent. The ﬁrst corresponds to the geodesic ﬂow,
while the second has gradient vanishing on the walls of the Weyl chambers.
